1. According to the United Kingdom, the Companies 
Act 1948 Does Not Support the Matimak Result 


As is pointed out in the UK ART 57 Brief, the Matimak 
court's reliance on the Companies Act 1948 was misplaced.° 
See UK ART 57 Brief, at 11-12. The court in Matimak 
apparently concluded that only corporations incorporated 
under that act could be considered a United Kingdom 
company. This view, however, confuses “Great Britain” with 
the “United Kingdom” and fails to recognize that each 
law district has an equivalent of the Companies Act.’ 
“The companies legislation of one law district is not superior 
to or more authentic than that of another.” See UK ART 57 
Brief, at 11. In fact, as the Diplomatic Service Procedure 
Manual indicates, all companies incorporated in any of the 
United Kingdom’s law districts are entitled to the full 
protection of the United Kingdom in their dealings with 
foreign states. /d. 


6. The Companies Act 1948 was replaced by the Companies 
Act 1985. See Companies Act, 1985, ch. 6 (Eng.). 


7. For example, companies in Northern Ireland, which is a 
separate law district, are incorporated not under the Companies 
Act 1985 but under the Companies (Northern Ireland) Order 1986, 
No. 1032, ch.6 (N.Ir.). See UK ART 57 Brief, at 12. 


288a 


Appendix G 


2. The BVI Attorney General Has Opined That BVI 
Corporations are Subjects of The United Kingdom 


This Panel also has the advantage of a statement from 
the British Virgin Islands’ Attorney General that fully 
supports the conclusion that residents and corporations of 
the British Virgin Islands are subjects of the United Kingdom. 
See UK ART 57 Brief, at Exhibit 3 (“On behalf of the 
Government of the British Virgin Islands, I fully support the 
arguments and conclusions presented in the [ART 57 
Properties| Brief of the United Kingdom.”). 


Under the appropriate standards, this Panel should give 
deference to the United Kingdom’s statement of who are its 
citizens and subjects, which is concurred in by the British 
Virgin Islands itself. It is axiomatic that the best interpreter 
of the laws of a foreign nation is the foreign nation itself. 
When a foreign land provides a court of the United States 
with that foreign nation’s interpretation of its own laws, that 
interpretation should be given great deference. /wanowa v. 
Ford Motor Co., 67 F.Supp. 2d 424, 490 (D. N.J. 1999). As 
was recognized by the Court in Matimak, a foreign nation is 
entitled to define who are its citizens and subjects, which 
should be recognized by other nations. See United States v. 
Wong Kim Ark, 169 U.S. 649, 668 (1898) (“Nor can it be 
doubted that it is the inherent right of every independent 
nation to determine for itself, and according to its own 
constitution and laws, what classes of persons shall be entitled 
to its citizenship.”); Murarka v. Bachrack Bros., 215 F.2d 
547, 553 (2d Cir. 1954) (“It is the undoubted right of each 
country to determine who are its nationals, and it seems 
to be general international usage that such a determination 


289a 


Appendix G 


will usually be accepted by other nations.”); see also 
Autocephalous Greek-Orthodox Church of Cyprus v. 
Goldberg & Feldman Fine Arts, Inc., 917 F.2d 278, 285 (7th 
Cir. 1990) (“[G]enerally accepted test for determining 
whether a person is a foreign citizen for purposes of 28 U.S.C. 
§ 1332(a)(2) is whether the country in which citizenship is 
claimed would so recognize him.”) 


To ignore such a statement would be tantamount to telling 
the government of the United Kingdom that a United States 
court is better suited to determine who its citizens and 
subjects are.* 


B. The Matimak Ruling Has Caused and Continues to 
Cause Foreign Entanglements with the United 
Kingdom 


Another important part of the Matimak analysis was the 
concept that because residents and corporations from the 
United Kingdom’s Overseas Territories were “stateless” there 
would be no problems of foreign policy entanglements. 
See Matimak Trading Co., 118 F.3d at 87 (“there is no danger 


8. In Favour Mind Ltd. v. Pacific Shores, Inc., 1999 WL 1115217 
(S.D.N.Y. Dec. 7, 1999), Judge Scheindlin, in addressing the issue 
of whether corporations from post-reversion Hong Kong were now 
residents of China, held that “this explicit statement by China compels 
this Court to find that Hong Kong SAR corporations are citizens of 
China and that Favour Mind may therefore invoke the alienage 
jurisdiction of the federal district courts.” /d. at *7. Judge Scheindlin 
went on to note as important that in Favour Mind, “unlike in Matimak, 
the three relevant governments — the United States, the Hong Kong 
SAR and, most important, China — all agree that Hong Kong SAR 
corporations are citizens of China. /d. at *7 n.12. 


290a 
Appendix G 


of foreign entanglements, as there is no sovereign with whom 
the United States could become entangled.”) Based on the 
submissions from both the United Kingdom and the United 
States Department of State, it is now evident that such a ruling 
has created entanglements with one of this country's closest 
allies. 


As the court in Matimak stated, “[t]he raison d’etre of 
alienage jurisdiction is to avoid ‘entanglements with other 
sovereigns that might ensue from failure to treat the legal 
controversies of aliens on a national level’.”” Matimak Trading 
Co., 118 F.3d at 82 (quoting Jran Handicraft & Carpet Exp. 
Ctr. v. Marjan Int'l Corp., 655 F. Supp. 1275, 1277 
(S.D.N.Y.), aff'd 868 F.2d 1267 (2d Cir. 1988)); see also 
Sadat v. Mertes, 615 F.2d 1176, 1186 (7th Cir. 1980) (“[T]he 
paramount purpose of the alienage jurisdiction provision [is] 
to avoid offense to foreign nations because of the possible 
appearance of injustice to their citizens.”). 


The result of Matimak, the treatment of residents and 
corporations from the United Kingdom’s Overseas Territories 
as “stateless” and, thus, denying them the ability to bring 
suit in Federal Court, has clearly created concern for the 
United Kingdom and embroiled the Department of State in 
an “entanglement” with the government of the United 
Kingdom.? The Matimak decision has created the very 


9. As Judge Sotomayor noted in her opinion dissenting from

purpose of alienage jurisdiction — to void offense to foreign nations 
— is frustrated by the Matimak decision and its further application

problem that alienage jurisdiction was designed to prevent, 
namely an international tension with a valued ally and

The fact that the United Kingdom is concerned over this 
issue is self-evident from the fact that the United Kingdom 
submitted the UK ART 57 Brief, the UK Matimak Brief and 
the UK III Finance Brief to the Courts of the United States. 
In addition, the United Kingdom has also issued a Diplomatic 
Note that specifically expressed the United Kingdom’s 
“concern about a number of judicial decisions which have 
denied corporations of United Kingdom Overseas Territories 
access to United States Federal Courts.” See Diplomatic Note 
No. 13/2000 from the British Embassy in Washington, D.C. 
to the United States State Department, Feb. 2, 2000 
at 1 (attached to the UK ART 57 Brief as Exhibit 1). 
The Diplomatic Note further stated: “The United Kingdom 
also views with great concern the potential application of 
the Matimak rationale to individual Overseas Territories 
residents, as well as to commercial enterprises.” /d. 


(Cont'd) 

by this panel.” 229 F.3d 187, 188 (2d Cir. 2000) (dissenting). As Judge 
Sotomayor pointed out, it is ironic that the nation that is offended by the 
ruling in Matimak, the United Kingdom, is “the very country the drafters 
of the alienage jurisdiction provision had in mind more than two hundred 
years ago when they sought to open the federal courts to foreign litigants.” 
Id. at 189. The Founding Fathers, recognizing the importance of 
continued commerce with the United Kingdom, included the concept of 
alienage jurisdiction due to a fear that state courts would not enforce the 
debts of British creditors after the Revolutionary War. See Kevin R. 
Johnson, Why Alienage Jurisdiction? Historical Foundations and 
Modern Justifications For Federal Jurisdiction Over Disputes Involving 
Noncitizens, 21 Yale J. Int’1 L. 1, 7-8 (1996). 


292a 


Appendix G 


It is clear that part of the concern about the Matimak 
decision by the United Kingdom comes from the belief that 
the United States is not providing comity in this instance. 
The Diplomatic Note states: 


The United Kingdom notes that all corporations 
validly incorporated under the laws of the United 
States and its territories are entitled to sue and be 
sued in the courts of the United Kingdom and its 
Overseas Territories. As a matter of international 
comity and reciprocity, the United Kingdom 
considers that United States Federal Courts should 
be open to corporations established in United 
Kingdom Overseas Territories. 


See UK ART 57 Brief, at Exhibit 1; see also UK ART 57 
Brief, at 21 (noting that corporations incorporated under the 
political subdivisions of the United States such as Delaware, 
Puerto Rico and American Samoa are entitled to bring suit 
in the United Kingdom even though these political 
subdivisions are not recognized by the United Kingdom as 
sovereign states). Any extension of the Matimak decision to 


the British Virgin Islands places in further jeopardy the ~ 


comity which is owed to the United Kingdom in this regard. 


C. The Department of State Agrees That BVI Residents 
and Corporations are Subjects of the United Kingdom 


The United States Department of State has also 
recognized the entanglement that has been created by the 
application of the Matimak decision and has actively asserted 
the position that residents and corporations from the United 


293a 


Appendix G 


Kingdom’s Overseas Territories should be deemed subjects 
of the United Kingdom. See US ART 57 Brief.'° 


It is no wonder that the Department of State agrees with 
the United Kingdom. At the international level, it is 
undisputed that the United Kingdom is responsible for the 
British Virgin Islands. For example, the United Kingdom not 
only negotiates international treaties on behalf of the British 
Virgin Islands, but also executes them on behalf of the British 
Virgin Islands. See, e.g., United Kingdom of Great Britain 
and Northern Ireland (on Behalf of the British Virgin Islands) 
and the United States of America: Exchange of Letter 
Constituting a Narcotics Cooperation Agreement with 
Respect to the British Virgin Islands, 1681 U.N.T.S. 451 
(1987). Thus, the United States recognizes the United 
Kingdom as the Sovereign over the British Virgin Islands. 


In addition, the terms of the Consular Convention 
between the United States and the United Kingdom provide 
the framework for consular relations between the two nations. 
Consular Convention, 3 U.S.T 3426, T.I.A.S. No. 2494 
(1952). The Consular Convention clearly states that it applies 
“to all territories over which the United States has jurisdiction 
or international responsibility and to all British Territories.” 
Treaties in Force as of Jan. 1, 1999 at 303 (1999): see also 


10. The State Department has also asserted its position in an 
amicus curiae brief that was submitted in Matimak (hereinafter “US 
Matimak Brief’) and in an amicus curiae brief to the United States 
Court of Appeals in the matter captioned Southern Cross Overseas 
Agencies, Inc. v. Wah Kwon Shipping Group Ltd., No. 98-5185 
(decision reported at 181 F.3d 410 (3rd Cir. 1999)) (hereinafter “US 
Southern Cross Brief”). The Southern Cross court upheld jurisdiction. 


294a 


Appendix G 


Consular Convention, Art. 1, Sec. (2), 3 U.S.T. at 3427 
(“Convention applies . . . on the part of his Majesty . . . to 
all His Majesty’s colonies.”) The Consular Convention also 
defines “nationals” of the United Kingdom as “all citizens of 
the United Kingdom and colonies ... including, where 
the context permits, all judicial entities duly created under 
the law of any of those territories.” Consular Convention, 
Art. 2, Sec. (4), 3 U.S.T. at 3428. Since the Consular 
Convention clearly shows that the United States government 
recognizes that the government of the United Kingdom is 
responsible for corporations created under the laws of the 
British Virgin Islands, the conclusion in Matimak, which is 
based on a contrary notion, is inconsistent with the position 
of the United States government, especially the executive 
branch. In this manner, the State Department’s position that 
residents and corporations of the United Kingdom’s Overseas 
Territories are subjects of the United Kingdom is clearly 
aligned with the dictates of the Consular Convention and is 
consistent with an attempt to avoid foreign policy difficulties 
with the United Kingdom. In such instances, when dealing 
with issues of foreign policy, the courts should give deference 
to the reasonable interpretation proffered by the Executive 
branch. See Jones v. United States, 137 U.S. 202, 212-14 
(1890); Regan v. Wald, 468 U.S. 222, 242 (1984); United 
States v. Diacolios, 837 F.2d 79, 83-84 (2d Cir. 1988); Tawain 
v. United States Dist. Court for the N. Dist. of Cal., 128 F.3d 
712, 718-19 (9th Cir. 1997).

D. The Issue Presented Raises Serious Concerns for the 
Commercial World 


. It is undisputed that the Overseas Territories of the United 
Kingdom, like the British Virgin Islands, Bermuda, the 
Cayman Islands and the islands of Turks and Caicos are 
emerging financial centers.'' As residents and corporations 
from these emerging markets contract with citizens of the 
United States, the Matimak decision clearly places 
uncertainty into these commercial relationships, especially 
in light of the fact that many of these contracts (like the 
Indenture in this case) call for a New York forum, including 
the possibility of suit in the United States District Courts in 


order to avoid any perceived prejudice a foreign party may 
encounter in state court. 


Because of these emerging and increasing financial/ 
economic relationships, the United Kingdom and the United 
States have taken a keen interest in the issue presented in 
Matimak and have asserted positions that are contrary to the

Kingdom and the United States have indicated to Chase’s

briefs on this issue in this case, either on a rehearing en banc 
or on a petition for certiorari to the Supreme Court. Since 
this issue is of such importance to not only the parties, but 
also the governments of the United States and the United 
Kingdom, Chase respectfully submits that to the extent this 
Panel feels constrained by Matimak, it should refer the matter 
to be heard en banc.

follow the decision in Peregrine Myanmar Ltd. rather than 
extend Matimak to the British Virgin Islands. In cases like 
this, which involve considerable controversy,’ there is no 
reason for this Panel to overrule a prior recent precedent.
